## Proposed outline
I'd keep your 10 headings so the section references in `requirements.md` still work. The page budget has to fit the 5-page cap, and the page counts are my estimates.

| § | Section | Pages | Covers |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| 1 | Context and capacity | 0.5 | Derived numbers only |
| 2 | Rendering, both apps | 0.5 | C1 |
| 3 | API layer and gateway | 0.5 | C2, F5 |
| 4 | Data access | 0.75 | C3, F1, F3 |
| 5 | Auth, roles, features | 0.5 | C4 |
| 6 | Dashboard fast and fresh | 0.75 | C5, F2, F4, F6 |
| 7 | Participant reliability | 0.5 | C6 |
| 8 | Trade-offs and rejections | 0.5 | C7 |
| 9 | Security, privacy, residency | 0.25 | F7; each component's impact stays in its own section |
| 10 | Risks and first tests | 0.25 | What I'd benchmark first |

**Candidate ADRs:**
- **0001:** analytics in Postgres only, or with a columnar store.
- **0002:** one stack per region, or one shared stack.
- **0003:** participant app as a static offline-first shell, or server-rendered.

## Top risks for each brief item

| Brief item | Top risks |
| --- | --- |
| **Rendering** | Next.js can ship too much JS for slow phones. Server rendering adds a round trip for every page on a bad network. RTL and language fonts add weight. |
| **API layer** | Long AI streams can tie up the same servers that handle quick writes, so a slow LLM could block answer saving. Inngest suits background work, not live token streaming. Gateway and CDN timeouts can also cut streams. |
| **Data access** | Combinable filters can't all be pre-computed, so rollups only cover unfiltered views. Read replicas can lag behind heavy writes, as Microsoft warns. A heavy cross-tab can also starve other users, because one big collection shares the same database. |
| **Auth** | Better Auth's organization plugin covers roles per organization, but I found no per-collection feature flags, so those are probably ours to build. A check missed on any one path (cache, export or chat) leaks data across clients. Participant identity must stay separate from answers. |
| **Fast and fresh** | A cached result can be shown to the wrong role or organization. 300 users can fire the same expensive query at once when a live update arrives. Summaries go stale and LLM costs grow during fieldwork. Large exports can also hurt live queries. |
| **Bad networks** | Retries can create duplicate answers unless writes are idempotent. A dropped AI stream can lose or repeat a turn. Answers stored offline on a shared phone expose health data. |
| **Trade-offs** | Staying on Postgres only may not survive the benchmark, and adding a second store is heavy for a small team. Each extra region multiplies ops work. |
| **Privacy (cuts across all)** | Narrow segments can identify a person, even by comparing two filters. Participant text can carry prompt injection into chat and summaries. Inngest, PostHog, Sentry and the LLM could all receive health data. |

## Where I disagree or see a gap
1. **Better Auth isn't a gateway.** It's an auth library, so routing and rate limiting need a real component (Q9).
2. **The CDN may break residency.** Front Door is a global service that decrypts traffic at its edge. I'd keep participant API traffic off it, even though TLS setup is slower on bad networks (Q10).
3. **The stack has no LLM.** Both apps need one, so I need your approval to add it (Q2).
4. **Kenyan data can't stay in Kenya on Azure.** "Nairobi" is only an example in the brief, but the only African regions Azure lists are in South Africa, and one of those is restricted. If in-country storage is required, Azure conflicts with it (Q1).
5. **"40M rows" is shakier than it looks.** It assumes one row per answer, but multiple choice adds rows and skipped questions remove them. If each response is one row, a filter scans at most 500k rows, which could keep Postgres viable. I'd benchmark that before relying on it (Q3).

## The blocking questions
Q1–3 have the widest effect, so answer those first if you're short on time.
1. **Residency:** which rules apply, and does each collection stay in one region?
2. **LLM:** which provider, and may data leave its region?
3. **Analytics store:** is Postgres a hard limit?
4. **Targets:** what do "fast" and "fresh" mean in numbers?
5. **AI engine:** do we own it, or does Derin already have one?
6. **Participant identity:** how do participants arrive and identify themselves?
7. **Group size:** what's the minimum for showing results, and does it apply to all roles?
8. **AI question format:** text only, or voice too?

## Still unverified
- **Regional models:** I don't know which current models South Africa North offers as regional deployments, because the docs results disagreed. This affects Q2, and I'll check it before any ADR relies on it.
- **Third-party hosting:** I haven't checked where Inngest, PostHog and Sentry host data (Q17).

## What your answers change

| Q | What changes |
| --- | --- |
| **Q1** | Each collection carries a region, and requests must be routed to it. In-country storage is out of scope. Where dashboard accounts live is now open (Q24). |
| **Q2** | Global, Data Zone and Batch deployments are all out, so summaries run as normal Inngest jobs. AI features must fail closed, which needs a capability check per region. |
| **Q3** | Section 4 is Postgres only, with a stated exit. As a design-only exercise, it names a trigger rather than a result. My proposed trigger is any Q4 target missed at 500k × 80 and the derived load, after tuning, on the largest suitable tier. |
| **Q4** | Tighter than my default: charts go from 30s to 10s. If a query takes 3s, cache age plus replica lag can be at most 7s (10 − 3, DERIVED from your assumed targets). Ad-hoc caching therefore helps little on busy collections. |
| **Q5** | The diagram shows an engine adapter rather than a fixed engine. Capacity and privacy need stating for both cases. |
| **Q6** | No participant accounts, so Better Auth covers dashboard users only. Resume on another device works through the link itself. |
| **Q7** | Suppression moves into one shared query layer that caches, cross-tabs, chat and exports all use. |
| **Q8** | The turn model stays independent of media type. South Africa North also lacks regional Whisper, which would constrain voice later. |

## Where I disagree

**1. Q7 conflicts with the brief (Q20).** One transcript or export row is a group of 1, so "every output" removes transcript browsing and row-level exports, which the brief requires.
- **Recommendation:** apply the minimum of 10 to all aggregates, including chat and summaries. Make transcripts and row-level exports a separate audited permission, reachable only from a filter matching at least 10, with identifiers redacted.

**2. "Stop people comparing filters" can't be fully guaranteed (Q21).** Exact counts plus free combination of filters can always leak something, and two live refreshes can be compared the same way. If one person arrives between refreshes, the difference can reveal their answers.
- **Recommendation:** commit to "reduce", with named controls: secondary suppression, complement checks and audit, plus rounded low counts.
- **Cost:** holding segment updates until 10 new responses arrive means small segments will miss your 10s freshness target.

**3. "Regional only" is stricter than Microsoft's commitment (Q23).** The [availability page](https://learn.microsoft.com/azure/foundry/foundry-models/concepts/models-sold-directly-by-azure-region-availability) says regional, but the [privacy page](https://learn.microsoft.com/azure/foundry/responsible-ai/openai/data-privacy) says processing may move between regions within the geography. Abuse-monitoring data can also be stored in that geography for possible human review.
- **Recommendation:** promise geography, not region, and keep conversation state in our own database. Apply for modified abuse monitoring, but approval is ASSUMED.

**4. Q2 has a bigger consequence than either of us assumed (Q22).** I agree with failing closed, and this is the most important finding. South Africa North has only embedding models on Standard/Regional. Chat there needs Regional Provisioned: gpt-4o (2024-05-13) or gpt-5.1, and I haven't checked cost or minimum capacity. A client wanting AI on African data therefore has three options:
- pay for reserved capacity in South Africa North
- pin the collection elsewhere, such as Sweden Central, which Q1 allows but moves the data to Europe
- run without AI features

**Recommendation:** design around a region variable and check model availability when a collection is created. Use Sweden Central as the sizing example, and price South Africa North as the harder case.

**5. Pseudonymous doesn't cover what people type (Q6, Q25).** Participants can write names and places into transcripts, so keeping identifiers separate doesn't protect that text. It also means transcripts need redaction, and any redaction service must stay in the region too. A signed link is also a bearer credential, so a forwarded link exposes an in-progress response, and a signature alone can't be revoked.
- **Recommendation:** exchange the link for a short-lived token, keep revocation state on the server, and don't show earlier answers again on a new device. This also needs your explicit approval as a stack exception, since participants would sit outside Better Auth.

**6. An interface alone doesn't settle Q5.** If Derin's engine is reused, these apps can't enforce your Q2 rule themselves.
- **Recommendation:** make the contract carry the region requirement, plus resumable, idempotent turns. Size capacity for the case where we own the engine, since that's the worst case for us.

**7. Q1 and Q4 have smaller gaps.**
- **Q1 (Q24):** pinning doesn't say where dashboard accounts live. I recommend a small global control plane with users, organizations, roles and the collection-to-region map, and no participant data.
- **Q4 (Q26):** the targets need definitions to be testable. I recommend measuring age from server commit, which excludes time in a phone's offline queue, and applying 3s per query rather than per 80-chart page.

I agree with Q3 and Q8 as written.

## What I need from you
1. Your answers to Q20–Q26, which are in section C of the file with my proposed defaults.
2. Your exceptions for Q9–Q19. I'd look hardest at Q10, Q15, Q16 and Q17: Q10, Q15 and Q16 interact with your answers, and I still haven't verified Q17's vendor hosting regions.

I also updated [assumptions.md](docs/assumptions.md) and [workflow-log.md](docs/workflow-log.md). I'm stopping here until you decide.

## What to check first
1. **The narrow table doesn't save writes (0001).** It writes more: 44 GB per collection against 40 GB for one row per response. A 2s batch holds about 0.3 answers per response even at burst, so batching merges almost nothing. I still recommend it, because saves stay small and safe to retry and the read table can be rebuilt. If you value simplicity more, one row per response is a fair choice.
2. **Local token verification is only partly confirmed (Q24).** The Better Auth docs confirm that tokens verify through JWKS with no verify call or database check. They only show `definePayload` with the user, not an org role claim, so a regional API can prove who someone is but not yet their role. That needs a spike (Q28).
3. **LLM capacity may be the hardest limit (0002).** Burst works out to 38.7M tokens per minute, based on my assumed turn sizes. I haven't checked this against Azure quota in any region, or priced provisioned capacity in South Africa North.

## Limits your decisions introduce
- **No gateway (Q9):** Front Door's rate limits count per IP by default, and phones on one carrier may share an IP. Thresholds therefore have to stay high, and NestJS carries the real limits. NestJS instances share no counters and the stack has no cache, so per-token limits are approximate (Q27).
- **No pgvector (Q15):** chat search falls back to Postgres full-text search, which is likely weak for some languages, including Arabic. I haven't tested that.
- **Freshness budget:** the projector takes 2s, so cache age plus replica lag must stay within 5s, not the 7s I gave earlier. Microsoft warns replica lag can grow under sustained heavy writes, so this is the most likely target to miss.

## Still unverified
Each of these is labelled in the ADRs or [assumptions.md](docs/assumptions.md):
- all capacity inputs and the Postgres targets, since nothing is benchmarked
- server-sent events through Front Door, which I found no page on and which should be tested first
- first load under 150 KB of JS, since bundle sizes aren't measured
- Inngest, PostHog and Sentry hosting regions
- which redaction service to use for free text, which isn't chosen yet

## Verdict
This is not a reviewable system design yet. It is three good but partial ADRs plus a capacity sheet.

- **Diagram not reviewed:** `diagram/architecture.png` and the `.excalidraw` source do not exist. `diagram/` holds only `README.md`, so I could not check components, flows, technology choices or agreement with the ADRs.
- **`docs/design.md`:** headings only. Its section 3 still says "gateway", which contradicts Q9.
- **ADRs 0001-0003:** all Proposed, and nothing is benchmarked.
- **`submission/ai-workflow-note.md`:** empty headings. `submission/transcript/` does not exist.
- **Dashboard:** the weakest area. It has no rendering decision, and cross-tabs, saved filters, exports, summary and chat have no design.

The ADRs are honest about their uncertainty. ADR 0003's reply delivery is the strongest thinking here.

**Not checked:** I had no Microsoft Learn or Better Auth access in this review, so all external citations are unverified by me.

**Arithmetic:** I recomputed every row in `docs/assumptions.md` and it is correct. The problems are in the model and labels, not the sums.

## 1. Brief items not covered or only claimed

| High | Executive summary | Degradation order only. No input, tokens, trigger or pipeline. | ADR 0002, "What degrades first" |
| High | Transcript browsing | Only claimed. Q20 requires redaction, but ADR 0003 says "Service not chosen". | ADR 0003, Privacy |
| High | Charts per question type | Partial. "Counters per question and option" does not fit numeric questions, which have no options. Bins, means and percentiles are undefined. | ADR 0001, Rollups |

## 2. Numbers without sound derivation

| Severity | Number | Problem | Where |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| High | 300 AI turns/s, 38.7M tokens/min | Contradicts the model's own inputs. See below. | `assumptions.md`, AI; ADR 0002, LLM capacity |
| High | 30 refresh reads/s | Polling every 10s uses the whole 10s age target before projector delay and lag. Push on each 2s version gives 300 / 2 = 150 reads/s, five times higher and synchronized. ADR 0001 uses push, the figure assumes polling. | `assumptions.md`, Dashboard; ADR 0001, Live updates |
| High | 70% cache hit, 12 cores | No basis, and the keying undercuts it. See weak point 2. | `assumptions.md`, Dashboard |
| High | gpt-4.1-mini quota rows | Excerpt only, and the plan depends on them. Sweden Central availability for this model is not recorded. See weak point 4. | `assumptions.md`, Quota and Models |
| Medium | A vs B WAL table | Option A's 160 B per-question versions are left out of its 1 KB row. See below. | ADR 0001, comparison |
| Medium | "Dead rows are the larger difference" | At the chosen design case, s = 1, both options produce 40M dead versions. The count difference is zero. Only the location differs. | ADR 0001, Total data written |
| Medium | WAL totals, 1.9 MB/s | Exclude transcripts, AI replies, rollups, full-text index and full-page images. Some exclusions are stated. Transcripts, replies and the search index are not. | `assumptions.md`, Total data written |
| Medium | 5s cache plus lag budget | 2s is the batch interval, not batch run time. A burst batch is 1,500 x 2 = 3,000 row updates plus counters. | `assumptions.md`, Dashboard |
| Low | "Time to write 40M at peak", 22 hours | 500/s is the average, not the peak, and is shared across the stack. With three collections sharing evenly, it is 40M / 167 = about 67 hours. | `assumptions.md`, Total data written |

**The 300/s contradiction:**
- Concurrency stays fixed, and only 2,000 participants are in AI questions. With a 10s stream, the maximum is 2,000 / 10 = 200 turns/s, even with no thinking time.
- The sheet caps open streams at 2,000 but does not cap the turn rate.
- 300/s needs a stream of 6.67s or less.
- At 200/s, the burst is 200 x 2,150 x 60 = **25.8M**, not 38.7M. gpt-4.1-mini Tier 3 (30M tokens, 30,000 requests) would then pass, which changes the written conclusion.
- **The other side:** 10s for 150 tokens is 15 tokens/s, which may be slow. A shorter stream makes 300/s possible. Either way, the stream length and the burst rate cannot both stand.

**The WAL correction:**
- With versions, A's high case is 40M x 1.21 KB = 48.4 GB, against 50 GB for B.
- The high-case gap at s = 1 falls from 8 GB to 1.6 GB, and break-even moves from 1.24 to 1.04.
- This helps your recommendation. But "A writes less" is overstated.
- **The other side:** A could drop versions, but then it loses stale-save protection.

**ASSUMED inputs with no reason given.** `assumptions.md` line 3 requires one: 1 KB, 100 B, 50 B, 10 KB, 2,000 and 150 tokens, 10s stream, 1s on 4 workers, 70%, 2s batch.

**Numbers only in ADRs, not in `assumptions.md`:** 160 B, 500 ms, 32 vCores, 80% reserve, 25s poll, 5s socket wait, 5-minute retry limit, 150 KB, 1,000 updates/s. These are labelled, so this is a rule breach rather than a hidden claim.

**No figure at all:**
- Infrastructure: primary vCores and IOPS, NestJS instances, replica count, PgBouncer pool, Redis size.
- Product load: summary tokens, export size and duration, first-load time on slow 3G, rollup updates per batch, multiple-choice rows, reconnection storm size.
- Lifetimes and growth: participant token lifetime, total collections, retained data growth.
- Cost: none anywhere.

## 3. The 5 weakest design points

### 1. Critical: the projector has no defined way to read new rows
**Where:** ADR 0001, Decision (Projector, Write table).

The ADR says it batches every 2s. It does not say how it finds new rows, resumes, or runs as a single owner.

**Failure:** the obvious cursor is "ID greater than last seen". A transaction holding a lower time-ordered ID commits after the projector has moved past it. That answer never reaches the read table or counters. No error fires, saves look healthy, and a client sees wrong health results.

Two running projectors, or a batch retried after a crash, add further risk unless the cursor commits in the same transaction.

**Fix:** decide on one of a per-row projected marker, a transaction-ID-safe watermark or logical decoding. Add a reconciliation job comparing write table and read table counts.

**The other side:** this could be called implementation detail. But the ADR names the projector as its main cost, and this is the first interview question I would ask.

### 2. Critical: the dashboard read path can serve stale data as current, and its capacity does not close
**Where:** ADR 0001 (Query cache, Live updates, Consequences); `assumptions.md`, Dashboard.

- **Cache poisoning:** the projector publishes version N from the primary. A dashboard queries a lagging replica and caches the old result under key N. When the survey goes quiet, no N+1 arrives. The old result then looks current until a TTL that the ADR never defines. The fix is to read the data version from the replica, in the same snapshot as the query.
- **Hit rate:** keying on data version resets the cache every 2s on a busy collection. At 10 queries/s, that is 20 queries per window across all users and filters. A 70% hit needs 14 of those 20 to repeat exactly. The ADR calls 70% "optimistic", but still states the 12-core result.
- **Cores:** the realistic case is near 40 cores, above the 32-vCore benchmark budget. The benchmark does not state whether the cache is on, or at what hit rate. A Redis outage therefore sends a replica past its budget, and that is the stated fallback.
- **Replica conflicts:** the read table is rewritten constantly, and 3s scans run on the replica. `assumptions.md` cites `hot_standby_feedback`, but no ADR chooses a setting. Either replay pauses and the 5s budget fails, queries get cancelled, or the primary bloats. The 500 MB scan size ignores that bloat.
- **Rollup location:** rollup reads have no stated server, primary or replica. If they use the primary, dashboards load the participant write server.
- **Q21 state:** "release only after 10 new responses" needs state per filter, and no store for it is defined.

### 3. High: nobody owns an AI turn that is in flight
**Where:** ADR 0003, "Getting the participant their reply".

Generation is "detached from the connection", but it still appears to run inside a NestJS process.

**Failures:**
- **Deploy:** ADR 0003 admits deploys drop sockets. If generation runs in those same processes, up to 2,000 in-flight turns die. The retry path covers model call failures, not process death. No lease, sweeper or timer owner is defined, so the turn may stay pending and the phone may long-poll indefinitely.
- **Retry versus idempotency:** "the same turn ID never creates a second model call" conflicts with re-running a dead turn. The rule needs a state machine.
- **Reconnect:** a socket that lands on another instance cannot receive tokens, because Redis must not carry content. The client gets the whole reply at the end. That is acceptable, but it is not stated as the normal deploy outcome.
- **Redis down:** ADR 0002 says "interviews continue". ADR 0003 wakes pollers through Redis and defines no fallback. Replies then wait up to the 25s poll hold, and ADR 0001's live updates also stop. Each ADR analyses only its own slice of Redis.
- **Content filter:** a participant in a health survey mentions self-harm. Azure's filter may refuse the turn. "Model call fails or is refused" then retries a deterministic refusal for 5 minutes, and the survey moves on. There is no separate path for it and no safeguarding response. That is a duty-of-care problem, as well as a reliability one.

### 4. High: AI interview capacity has no confirmed model, quota or tier
**Where:** ADR 0002 (Models, LLM capacity); `assumptions.md`, Quota; Q33, Q35.

- **Availability:** the plan sizes on gpt-4.1-mini. `assumptions.md` records only gpt-4.1 and gpt-5.1 for Sweden Central Standard/Regional, so the model's availability is not evidenced. Its retirement date is not recorded either.
- **Quota source:** the tier table comes from an excerpt. 225M tokens per minute on a regional Standard deployment looks high to me. I could not verify it. If those figures are for Global Standard, the table is unusable because Global is excluded.
- **Undecided headline:** the bold 7.6M and the "Reaches 7.6M?" column depend on Q33 and Q35, which are not decided. At the owner's accepted Q11 figures, the comparison should be against 38.7M, or 25.8M if corrected.
- **Worked failure:** on Tier 2 with no Q35 cap, 12M x 80% = 9.6M. 9.6M / 2,150 / 60 = about 74 turns/s, below even the 100/s average. Across 2,000 participants, that is about 27s per turn. The degradation order has no thresholds, so it cannot say when step 3 starts.
- **Region:** the sizing uses Sweden Central, the easy case. The brief's own example is Nairobi. South Africa North has only unpriced Provisioned chat. So the likeliest African client has no known cost, no sizing and possibly no AI questions, or data pinned to Europe with latency no one has measured.

**The other side:** failing closed is correct under Q2 and Q22. The objection is to treating the example region as the design case, not to the rule.

### 5. High: the invite link and resume design leaks data and can lose answers
**Where:** ADR 0003 (Privacy, Flow, Consequences); ADR 0001 (Retry row); Q25.

- **Pending reply:** a participant forwards the link or leaves it on a shared phone. The next holder opens it and sees the pending reply. ADR 0003 allows this because it "isn't one of the participant's answers". But an interview reply can repeat the answer, for example "You mentioned your HIV treatment...". This defeats the purpose of Q25.
- **Skip logic:** it runs on the phone. On a new device, or after storage is cleared, earlier answers are withheld. The phone then cannot evaluate skip logic based on those answers, or the server must send them, which breaks Q25. Neither ADR resolves this.
- **Version collision:** the new device restarts versions. Its changed answer can match an existing response, question and version key. The unique key treats a genuine conflict as a retry, so one answer is silently dropped. Separately, a Postgres unique key on a partitioned table must include `collection_id`, and the ADR's key does not.
- **Stored invite token:** it may sit in local storage on a shared phone for offline re-exchange. The participant token lifetime is undefined.
- **In-app browsers:** links opened inside WhatsApp or similar browsers, and browser storage eviction, are not considered. These are likely paths for this audience. I have not tested their behaviour.

**The other side:** a forwarded link is partly accepted by Q25. The pending reply exposure is new, though, and the owner has not explicitly accepted it.

## 4. Decisions lacking a rejected alternative

| Severity | ADR | Decision with no real alternative recorded |
| --- | --- | --- |
| High | 0001 | Projector mechanism. Only Inngest is dismissed, in passing. Triggers, logical decoding and marked rows are absent. |
| High | 0001 | Push on data version versus polling or TTL cache |
| High | 0001 | Rollup counters updated by the projector, versus cubes by screener, per-option bitmaps, or sampling |
| High | 0001 | Central suppression layer versus database-enforced views |
| High | 0001, 0003 | Dashboard rendering: no decision and so no alternatives |
| High | 0002 | Single-region control plane, versus HA or a regional session copy |
| High | 0002 | 5-minute token, versus a revocation list in Redis |
| High | 0003 | In-process detached generation, versus a separate worker or durable queue. Only Inngest streaming is rejected. |
| Medium | 0001 | Search: pgvector was declined by the owner, with no reason recorded and no other search option considered |
| Medium | 0001 | Partition by collection, and reading from a replica rather than the primary |
| Medium | 0002 | Provisioned versus Standard as the base case; 80% reserve and degradation order |
| Medium | 0002 | Inngest cloud versus self-hosted; Front Door Premium versus Standard. Premium is noted but not decided. |
| Medium | 0002 | Participants outside Better Auth: Q6 mentions the anonymous plugin, but the ADR's Rejected table does not |
| Medium | 0003 | Next.js versus a lighter client (stack rule aside), and a server-rendered first page with a hydrated shell. The tempting hybrid is missing from a comparison that calls first load "the biggest bet". |
| Medium | 0003 | Token in first socket message versus a short-lived socket ticket |
| Low | 0002 | "My Q9 decision" and "My Q10 decision" as rejection reasons. Both do give trade-offs, but neither quantifies the benefit. The split TCP gain has no number. |

## 5. Disagreements between documents

| Severity | Conflict | Scenario or consequence | Where |

**Residency, ranked separately (High, arguable).** Q10 is the owner's decision, so this is not a contradiction. But ADR 0002 records that WAF managed rules inspect request bodies at the edge. That is harder to call "in transit" than plain forwarding, especially since it can happen outside the geography. If a client's lawyer rejects the reading, the fix is a second entry point, which ADR 0003 also rejected. For Kenyan data pinned to Sweden, cross-border rules will likely come up, even if you claim no specific law. **The other side:** the risk is labelled ASSUMED, and split TCP has real value on these networks. Keep the decision if you want, but state a client-facing position and a fallback.

## Your three challenges

**0001: Was 44 vs 40 GB just final storage?** No. Those were row bytes written over a collection's life; final storage is 0.5 GB vs 6.5 GB. They also left out WAL, indexes and rewrites, so I rebuilt the comparison in WAL per collection (all sizes assumed):

| Saves per answer | One row, high | Narrow + read, high | One row, low | Narrow + read, low |
| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
| 1 (no server autosave) | 42 GB | 50 GB | 8 GB | 16 GB |
| 1.5 | 63 GB | 54 GB | 12 GB | 20 GB |
| 3 | 126 GB | 66 GB | 24 GB | 32 GB |
| 5 | 210 GB | 82 GB | 40 GB | 48 GB |

The recommendation still holds, but not on data volume. With autosave kept on the phone, one row per response writes less. I still prefer the narrow table for safe retries and being able to rebuild the read table. The reviewer also found I left the per-question versions out of the one-row option, which narrows the high-case gap at one save from 8 GB to 1.6 GB (finding 13).

**0002: How is 38.7M split?** It's 36.0M input and 2.7M output, so 93% is input. You were right that it looks high, and the reviewer showed it's wrong: 2,000 participants with a 10s stream can't exceed 200 turns/s, which gives 25.8M, not 38.7M (finding 4).
- **What brings it down most:** capping input from 2,000 to 800 tokens (56%) and a slower AI pace of 45s (56%). Together they save 80%. Shortening replies saves only 2%.
- **What degrades first, in order:** summary refresh, then dashboard chat, then new AI interviews waiting at the start, then shorter context and fewer follow-ups (flagged in the data), then delayed replies.
- **What never happens:** data never goes to another geography, and a saved answer is never lost.

**0003: What if SSE doesn't work?** It doesn't: Microsoft's Front Door page says it "doesn't support Server-Sent Events", and I missed that earlier. I redesigned around it, and the transport is yours to confirm (Q32):
- **Durable reply:** the reply is saved to Postgres, independent of any connection.
- **Delivery paths:** WebSocket first, then long-polling over ordinary HTTP, then the reply waits for when the participant returns.

| 2 | No path for a content filter refusal | A participant mentions self-harm, the turn is refused, retried for 5 minutes, then skipped, with no safeguarding response | 0003 |

No subagent has reviewed this change, and nothing is committed.

## What happens now
All timings are assumed starting values, to tune in testing.

| | Slow | Down | Over quota |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| **How we know** | No first token within 5s, or reply unfinished at 30s | Half of calls failing over 30s | Azure returns 429, or the Redis budget is nearly used |
| **Server does** | One model call only, stopped at 60s; sheds dashboard AI first | Stops calling the model, probes every 30s, queues turns | Waits as long as Azure asks, then follows the degradation order |
| **Participant mid-turn sees** | Typing indicator, then "Still working, your answer is saved" at 10s | "Your answer is saved, and the reply is coming"; can close and return with the link | Same as slow, then same as down |
| **New AI interview** | Starts normally | Waits, with an option to return later | Waits, with an option to return later |
| **Dashboard** | Charts unaffected; chat slower, then limited | Charts unaffected; chat unavailable; last summary with an "as of" time | Charts unaffected; chat limited or paused; summary paused |

- **Always true:** deterministic questions and saves keep working, and a participant's text is saved before any model call. Data never leaves the geography, and affected turns are marked in the data.
- **Unanswered turns:** after 5 minutes, a turn is marked unanswered and the survey moves on. An AI question reached during an outage is marked not asked, and researchers can filter these out.
- **Degradation order:** summary refresh pauses first, then chat, then new AI interviews wait. After that, sessions in progress get shorter context, and replies are delayed last.
- **Recovery:** traffic returns gradually, oldest waiting turns first. New interviews resume next, and dashboard AI last.

**The main cost to accept:** if models are down across a region, AI questions stop there until they recover, because Q2 rules out failover. The ADR now says this openly. I also added rejected alternatives: failing over to another geography, canned questions, blocking the whole survey, and retrying slow calls in parallel.

## What else changed
- **The 38.7M figure is gone (finding 4).** I couldn't keep a number the reviewer had shown to be wrong, so I capped AI turns at 200/s in [assumptions.md](docs/assumptions.md) and [ADR 0003](docs/adr/0003-participant-offline-first-shell.md). This was the one finding I acted on, because your change removed that number anyway.
- **Q33 and Q35 are reworded and still open.** For Q33, I now suggest closing it, since pace only affects the rough estimate. Q35 now asks what makes a model suitable for a region, which still decides whether that region can run AI questions.
- **[workflow-log.md](docs/workflow-log.md)** has a new entry for this change.

## Still open

- **Finding numbers in the ADR.** The ADR refers to findings 2 and 5 by their ranking in this chat, which no file records. I'll reword that when those are fixed.
- **Other findings.** Everything else stays unfixed, pending your review.

## What changed

| Item | Before | Now |
| --- | --- | --- |
| **Q9** | No gateway: Front Door plus NestJS | Regional Application Gateway with WAF for all API traffic |
| **Q10** | APIs through Front Door, uncached | Front Door for static files only, so participant data is decrypted only in the region and the assumed in-transit reading is gone |
| **Q32** | Open, with WebSocket proposed | SSE for AI turns and chat, plus saved reply and long-polling; dashboard live updates poll |
| **ADR 0002** | Front Door handled routing and limits | Gateway layout, buffering and timeout tables, connection cost, and WebSocket through Front Door recorded as rejected |
| **ADR 0003** | WebSocket, then long-poll | SSE, then long-poll, then return later; streams close at token expiry |
| **assumptions.md** | Front Door sources | Gateway sources, settings and connection cost added |
| **Q36 (new, open)** | Not asked | Should each gateway have DDoS Protection? WAF doesn't stop volumetric attacks, and API traffic has lost Front Door's edge. |

## What Microsoft's docs say

**Does turning off response buffering limit the WAF? No.** Microsoft states "Response buffering isn't impacted by the WAF." There are still three catches:
- The WAF always buffers requests, which is fine for small answers.
- The setting applies to the whole gateway, so every response becomes unbuffered, not just streams.
- Buffering is what protects NestJS from slow clients, so a slow phone can now hold a connection longer.

**What the timeouts mean for streams:**

| Timer | Value | What we do |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Backend request timeout | Default 20s, set per backend setting | 90s on stream and long-poll paths; the default would also have killed our 25s long-poll |
| Retry on timeout | v2 tries a second backend | The same turn ID never starts a second model call |
| HTTP/2 idle | 180s, fixed | 15s heartbeat during streams; a phone idle for longer reconnects |
| Connection draining | 30s by default; a gateway configuration update ends connections after it | Set above 90s; a cut stream falls back to the saved reply |
| Autoscaling | New instances take 3–5 minutes | Set minimum instances for peak load |

Microsoft's two pages describe the request timeout differently: one as the gap between events, the other as the wait for a response. I chose 90s because it sits above the 60s model stop, so it holds either way. It's still on the test-first list. The 90s and 15s are my assumed values.

**The connection cost:** each new connection takes 3–5 round trips over the full path. That adds about 300–500 ms to South Africa North and 600–1,000 ms to Sweden Central. Those use assumed round-trip times, not measurements. Participants on bad networks pay it most, because they reconnect most, but the shell's first load still comes from the edge.

## New risks
- **The WAF can block real answers.** Microsoft documents false positives on JSON and free text. The ADRs say to tune rules first, keep a blocked answer in the outbox, and alert rather than retry.
- **Gateway availability and cost.** I haven't checked availability in South Africa North, or gateway and DDoS Protection cost.

## Still out of step
You didn't list these, so I left them. They now contradict your decision:
- **[ADR 0001](docs/adr/0001-postgres-write-and-read-tables.md):** live updates still say push through Redis over WebSocket. Polling also raises a question: a 10s poll uses up the whole 10s freshness target, so 5s looks more realistic, which is 60 reads/s rather than 30.
- **`CLAUDE.md` and `brief.md`:** both still say "no separate gateway" and "Front Door routes". Surgo's text would stay untouched.

# Privacy and security review: ADRs 0001-0005

**Result:** the architecture boundaries are sound, but the disclosure controls are not. Three paths recover hidden health data with ordinary product features: differencing, row-level export and transcript browsing. There is also no design for identity separation or for prompt injection in interviews.

**Reviewed:** brief, ADRs 0001-0005, risks, open questions and assumptions. Nothing was edited.

**Not verified:**
- I had no MCP or web access, so Better Auth and Azure facts are taken from assumptions.md.
- Sentry and PostHog SDK behaviour in finding 6 comes from my general knowledge. Check it against vendor docs.
- "No section exists" claims come from reading the ADRs and from greps across docs/*.md.

**Files (all under ~/Desktop/derin-system-design/docs/):**
- adr/0001-postgres-write-and-read-tables.md
- adr/0002-regional-stacks-and-front-door.md
- adr/0003-participant-offline-first-shell.md
- adr/0004-dashboard-rendering-api-and-queries.md
- adr/0005-chat-and-executive-summary.md
- risks.md, open-questions.md, assumptions.md, design.md

## Critical

### 1. Exact counts on large segments let anyone recover suppressed groups (R2's controls fail here)
**Scenario:** a viewer wants a village with 3 respondents, which shows "too few responses".
1. They query `city in [nairobi, village]`, then `city in [nairobi]`, and pull the health question's chart for each.
2. Both segments are over 100, so counts are exact, because rounding applies only under 100.
3. The complement check compares against the total, so it doesn't fire. Each cell is over 10, so the cell minimum doesn't fire.
4. Subtracting gives all 3 people's answers. Two requests repeat this for each of the 80 questions.

**Means leak too:** the mean is hidden only under 10 and isn't rounded. For numeric questions, `count x mean` differenced across segments gives a person's exact value, such as a lab result.

**Chat makes it easier:** the 5-tool-call cap is per question, and the two-queries-per-user limit caps concurrency, not volume. Neither stops a two-query attack. The model can also do the subtraction, and the number check only marks the result.

**Change:** ADR 0004, "Suppression and comparison controls"; ADR 0005, "Keeping answers honest"; T4 in risks.md.
- Require each value in an ORed clause to meet the minimum of 10 on its own. Range edges need the same check.
- Round counts at every segment size, and round means to a coarse step, or derive them from rounded data.
- Block or mark derived numbers in chat that aren't direct tool results.
- Add this exact attack to T4.

**This challenges Q21 (yours):** "reduce" can stand, but this attack needs no outside knowledge or skill, so the current controls don't deliver even "reduce". Reconsider the rejected noise option too.

**The other side:**
- Rounding at n≥100 costs little precision, but cross-tabs then won't sum exactly and won't match row-level exports.
- Clause checks don't stop every overlap.
- Outside knowledge is still needed to tie a result to a named person, though a rare village narrows that a lot.

### 2. Row-level export defeats the minimum of 10, and the "can't be joined" claim is false
**Scenario:** an admin exports filter F (n=11), then F minus one village (n=10). Each row holds up to 80 coded answers, so the rows match on content, whatever the "random ID per export". The difference is one person's full health record.

Even a single export can contain someone unique on age, gender and location. The admin can also compute every suppressed cell locally. Rows may also follow time-ordered read-table order, which can line up with invite send times.

**Change:** ADR 0004, "Exports", and R4's scope. Before export:
- Bin the screener fields, and check that each combination of those fields has at least 10 rows.
- Leave out raw numeric values and timestamps.
- Shuffle row order.
- Limit overlapping exports per collection, or notify other admins.
- Require a second approver.
- Correct the join claim.
- Define whether "coded answers" includes the raw numeric value, because ADR 0001 stores one.

**This challenges Q16 and Q20 (yours):** the brief says "exports", not row-level exports. A k-anonymised export could meet the need.

**The other side:** researchers may need microdata for their own analysis, and the client may already own the data. If so, say plainly that the minimum of 10 doesn't protect against admins. R4 covers recall only, and doesn't cover this.

### 3. Analysts can rebuild row-level data through transcripts, though row export is admin only
**Scenario, rebuilding a record:**
1. References change only "between sessions", so they stay stable within one.
2. An analyst runs one filter per answer value, each matching 10 or more, and notes which references appear.
3. This rebuilds a transcript's closed answers and joins them to its story.

**Scenario, narrowing by search:** the floor of 10 applies to the filter, not to filter plus search terms, in both `transcripts/search` and `search_transcripts`. A filter matching 200 plus the term "Kibera clinic" can return one person.

**Scenario, bulk copying:** pagination has no volume limit, so transcript export's admin-only rule can be bypassed page by page. The 5-minute stale access window adds to this.

**Change:** ADR 0004, "Transcripts and redaction"; ADR 0005, tools table; R2.
- Issue references per result list, and never reuse them across filters.
- Apply the floor after search terms, and hide exact match counts.
- Set a daily transcript read limit per user.
- Make transcripts a real separate grant. R2 and ADR 0004 call it a separate permission, but the matrix bundles it into the analyst role.

**The other side:** per-query references hamper "open the source of this quote", though a short-lived link for each quote would solve that. Analysts may also be trusted researchers under contract, which would justify softer limits, but then it should be stated.

## High

### 4. Identity separation isn't designed, and consent and withdrawal have no flow
Q6 says identifiers are kept separate, but no ADR says:
- who holds the invite-to-contact list
- whether it's in our system or only the client's
- what the signed token contains
- where the invite-to-response mapping lives for revocation (Q25)
- who can read that mapping

**Scenario:** the client sends links from its patient list and keeps the token-to-patient record. The token's subject matches a response ID, or an operator or support export joins them. Names then attach to health answers, and "pseudonymous" in ADR 0001 is untrue.

**Also missing:**
- A consent step, with notice that an AI is asking the questions.
- Any way to start a withdrawal. ADR 0001 begins with a known response ID, but a participant with no account and a lost link has no route, and a client-side request needs the very mapping that should be kept apart.
- An expiry for invite links.

**Change:** add an "Identity and consent" section to ADR 0003, and a matching row to ADR 0002's privacy table. Fill in design.md sections 5 and 9. Suggested shape: a separate store with its own credential, a random invite ID never derived from the response ID, a one-way mapping, and a withdrawal code shown to the participant.

**The other side:** if the client alone holds contacts, we hold no direct identifiers, which is a good outcome. It still needs stating, and the client can then re-identify anyone whose token it can tie to a response.

### 5. Prompt injection in interviews isn't addressed
ADR 0005's threat table covers chat only. ADR 0003 covers only the content filter, which is not an injection control.

**Scenarios:**
- A participant, or someone with a forwarded link, gets the model to reveal the research guide or prompt, or to give dosing advice in a health survey. Replies stream with no output rule beyond the filter.
- A model reply includes a link, and ADR 0003 doesn't say how replies render. A participant could be sent to a phishing page.
- Injected text is stored, then reaches analyst chat through `search_transcripts`. ADR 0005 wraps "quotes", but doesn't say whether model-written turns are included. The tool limits contain data access, but not false conclusions, since the number check covers numbers only.

**Change:** add an "Interview injection and output rules" section to ADR 0003, and add these to the `ConversationEngine` contract:
- context limited to one response
- no tools
- no secrets in the prompt
- no clinical advice
- replies rendered as plain text, with no links
- transcript pages also rendered as plain text
- a CSP for the participant shell, which currently has none, even though it may hold the invite token and clear-text answers

A Derin engine must commit to these too.

**The other side:** the impact is mostly confined to the attacker's own session, so a short section is enough. Stating the "one response only" limit is the key part, and it's currently unstated.

### 6. Bearer secrets and identifying metadata can reach Sentry and PostHog
"No content" doesn't cover credentials and URLs, so R11's mitigation is incomplete.

**Scenarios:**
- **Invite token:** the exchange fails on a bad network, which is the most likely first error. Sentry loads and sends the full page URL including the fragment. Scrubbers don't recognise an opaque signed token, so a bearer credential to a health response now sits with a vendor in an unverified region. PostHog pageviews capture the same URL if they run before the fragment is cleared. (SDK behaviour unverified, as noted above.)
- **Export SAS URL:** a SAS URL in a fetch or navigation breadcrumb or link click event sends a 15-minute link to a row-level file to the vendor.
- **Participant metadata:** ADR 0003 says PostHog is off "answer screens" only. Other screens therefore send IP, device and collection, which reveal that this person is in a health survey. Sentry can do the same unless IP capture is off.
- **Backend errors:** nothing specifies how NestJS errors stay clean. ADR 0005's "no prompts" is a statement, not a mechanism. Postgres and Azure SDK errors can carry values.

**Change:** ADR 0003, "Budget" and privacy table; ADR 0004, "Analytics and errors"; ADR 0002, privacy table.
- Remove PostHog from the participant app entirely. This tightens Q17 (yours) from "no answers" to "no participant app".
- Clear the fragment before any SDK loads.
- Use an allowlist `beforeSend` that strips URLs, query strings and breadcrumbs.
- Turn off IP capture.
- Explicitly disable PostHog replay, which is currently stated for Sentry only.
- Don't put filter state in dashboard URLs.

**The other side:** funnel data on drop-off is valuable. It can come from first-party server counts in the region, so the cost is low.

### 7. "Access to the collection" is undefined, and the token can't express it
ADR 0004 says saved sets are visible to those "in the org with access to the collection", but the token carries only org and role, per the brief's per-org roles. Nothing states that the guard compares the token's org with the collection's org. A role is also bound to one active org in the session.

**Scenario:** a user who is admin in org A and viewer in org B calls org B's collection with an org A token. If the guard checks only the role, they get admin rights, including transcripts.

The brief also names "researchers and client organizations". A researcher at one org working on a client's collection doesn't fit the model. The row-level security check is ASSUMED, and appears only in a "Rejected" row.

**Change:** ADR 0004, "Roles and features"; ADR 0002, "Dashboard tokens"; T2.
- Add an explicit org-to-collection check.
- Decide about per-collection grants, and how they get to the region.
- Move row-level security into the ADR 0001 decision table.
- Log this in open-questions.md, since it can change the token design.

**The other side:** if one org always owns each collection, one explicit check is enough. It still needs writing down and testing.

### 8. The comparison detection can't work as described, and it alerts the wrong person
The audit stores a filter hash, and hashes of near-identical filters look unrelated. So "many near-identical filters" can't be found from what's stored. The alert also goes "to an admin", who may be the person doing it, and who already has row-level export.

**Scenario:** the finding 1 attack runs for a week. Each audit row has a different hash, and nothing fires.

**Change:** ADR 0004, "Audit" and "Detection".
- Store the validated filter structure. The audit table is already regional, and saved sets store the same values.
- Define a concrete measure, such as filter pairs differing by one clause value with results under N apart.
- Send alerts to all admins and to Surgo.
- Add role changes, feature changes and member management to the audit list, which currently omits them. Control-plane changes need a home too.

**The other side:** storing filters makes the audit table more sensitive. Restricted access and the 1-year limit handle that.

### 9. No MFA or SSO requirement for accounts that can export health data
A grep of docs/*.md for MFA, two-factor, 2FA, SSO, passkey and password finds nothing. The only stated gate on row-level export is the token and a live role check.

**Scenario:** a phished admin password yields a new session and a 500k x 80 export, and the file can't be recalled. An attacker can also add members.

**Change:** ADR 0002, "Dashboard tokens"; design.md section 5. Require MFA for admins and analysts, plus recent re-authentication for row-level and transcript export. Check what Better Auth offers through its MCP before naming any plugin.

**The other side:** clients may require SSO instead, which is an acceptable way to meet the same requirement.

### 10. Chat's "identity from the token" fails on detached and re-run turns
ADR 0005 runs tools with the user's token identity. But generation is detached from the connection (ADR 0003), and a turn can be re-run from `received` for up to 5 minutes. A new instance has no token, unless the token is stored, which would be worse, or claims are copied onto the turn row.

**Scenario:** an analyst is demoted while a turn is waiting. The sweeper re-runs it with the old role and calls `search_transcripts`, breaking R16's 5-minute limit. "Streams close at expiry" stops delivery, not generation. Afterwards, the saved answer and its quotes can still be fetched from history.

**Change:** ADR 0005, "Flow" and "History"; ADR 0003, "Who owns a turn".
- Store user, org, role and claim expiry on the turn.
- Refuse tool calls after expiry, and mark the turn for retry.
- Recheck the current role and turn ownership on every history and `GET .../{turn}` read.
- Hide quotes from a user who no longer holds the role. Summaries of earlier answers can also carry those quotes forward.

**The other side:** the window is short and the data was already visible to that user. A simple expiry check is still cheap.

## Medium

### 11. Live comparison control has holes
- **Equivalent filters:** held values are per filter. Adding `gender in [all options]` produces a new hash and so a fresh, unheld value, and no filter normalisation is specified.
- **Segments of 100 or more, and unfiltered live charts:** these aren't held at all. On a slow day, one new response between 5s polls shows one person's answers as the counters change. Anyone who knows when that person responded, such as a clinic worker, can use this.
- **Empty Redis:** "everything can be rebuilt" doesn't hold for release state. After a flush, ADR 0002's "never release early" has nothing to compare against.

**Change:** ADR 0004, "Live comparison"; ADR 0002, "If Redis is down". Normalise filters semantically, and hold results by the set of matched responses rather than the filter text. Release unfiltered charts only after a minimum batch of new responses. After a flush, show "too few responses" until state is rebuilt.

**The other side:** batching unfiltered charts can break the 8s freshness target when responses are slow. Small-segment exemption (Q21) is the precedent. At high response rates, batches form quickly, so the cost is small.

### 12. Other suppression gaps
- **Linked tables:** secondary suppression works per table. A hidden cross-tab cell can be recovered from one-way charts on the same filter (`/views`).
- **Small hidden sums:** two hidden cells of 1 and 1 still give a sum of 2 from the total. The hidden total must reach 10 too.
- **Identical answers:** if all 15 women in a segment choose "positive", no cell is under 10, yet membership reveals the answer. No control covers this.
- **Toggle difference:** "all" minus "completed" may leave a group under 10, and the complement check doesn't cover it.
- **Second implementation:** the projector writes "suppressed rollups" (ADR 0001), and Redis-down polls read a replica (ADR 0002). Both sit outside the "one path" claim in ADR 0004.

**Change:** ADR 0004, suppression table and "Cross-tabs"; ADR 0001, "Read path". All paths must call one suppression function.

**The other side:** suppressing identical answers hides valid findings, such as "all respondents said yes". A warning or a threshold for sensitive questions only may be enough.

### 13. A forwarded link can write answers, and branching can reveal them
**Scenario:** a partner opens a forwarded link. The server returns the next question ID and branch results. If the next question is "How long have you been on treatment?", the earlier answer is disclosed. They can also submit new answers, since across devices the last one received wins, which corrupts the data.

ADR 0003 calls this "a smaller exposure", but in a health survey it may be the most sensitive fact. The invite token also stays in local storage to allow re-exchange.

**Change:** ADR 0003, "Resuming on a new device". Bind the link to the first device, and require a step-up to resume elsewhere, such as a short code given at the start. Block overwriting from a new device. This challenges Q25 (yours).

**The other side:** participants on shared or replaced phones lose easy resume, and step-ups hurt completion. Allowing a restart with no history, and treating it as a new submission, could be a fair middle ground.

### 14. Clear-text answers on shared phones have no expiry
Q13 says local answers are deleted after sync. Nothing covers answers that never sync, including those blocked by WAF false positives (R8), which "stay in the outbox" indefinitely. There's no expiry or "clear this device" action.

**Change:** ADR 0003, privacy table and "Abandoned phones". Add an expiry on the outbox and the stored invite token, an explicit clear action, and a route for WAF-blocked answers. Requiring the invite link again to view queued items would also help.

**The other side:** expiry loses answers from people offline for a long time. Choose a limit based on how long the survey stays in the field.

### 15. First-party logs and internal access are undefined
No section covers:
- **Gateway access logs:** these can hold IP, time and collection or turn paths, which links a person to a health survey.
- **Postgres logs:** statement or error logs may carry parameters.
- **Log retention:** no period is set for any log, and only WAF logs have a stated location.
- **Operator access:** there's no rule for who at Surgo can read raw data, and no break-glass process.
- **Network isolation:** private networking for Postgres, Redis and Azure Language isn't mentioned, and a grep for private endpoint, VNet and Key Vault finds no design decision. NestJS hosting is also not named, though ADR 0002's timeout limit depends on it.
- **Database roles:** "Nothing else can read the read table" and "Only interview generation reads raw" have no enforcement. Grants also apply on replicas. The second claim is false as written, since the redaction job reads raw text too.

**Scenario:** a bug in any NestJS module, or an engineer with the shared DB credential, reads raw transcripts on a replica. Separately, a log query joins participant IPs to turn paths.

**Change:** add an "Internal access and logs" section to ADR 0002, plus design.md section 9. Use separate database roles for interview, redaction, query and export, with no replica grant on raw tables.

**The other side:** a small team can't run heavy process. One break-glass role with logging and a short log retention covers most of it.

### 16. Inngest "IDs only" is weaker than R11 assumes
- **Volume contradiction:** ADR 0001 rejects per-answer events on volume and content grounds, but ADR 0004 runs redaction as "an async job after each AI answer", at about 100 per second (DERIVED in assumptions.md). The vendor then gets a per-participant activity timeline, and time-ordered IDs carry timing as well.
- **Withdrawal:** the rollup job may tell the vendor which response withdrew, though ADR 0001 only says "IDs only".
- **Failures:** "IDs only" covers events and return values, but not thrown errors or retries. A Postgres or model error could carry content into the vendor's run history. Storage is unverified (R11).
- **Reach:** the vendor's call path into each regional stack isn't described. Whether one Inngest account spans regions isn't stated either. ADR 0002 also lists Inngest functions under the regional stack's "all participant data", which understates the vendor's position.

**Change:** ADR 0002, layout and privacy tables; ADR 0004, "When". Drive redaction from a Postgres-backed worker, as the projector does. Use batch IDs with collection-level jobs, opaque job IDs and sanitised errors. Use one Inngest environment per region, with verified signing.

**The other side:** self-hosting could remove most of this, but with more operational load. If only opaque IDs reach it, keeping the cloud service is defensible once its terms are verified.

### 17. Withdrawal and retention are incomplete
- **Withdrawal misses:** chat history (30 days of redacted quotes) isn't in ADR 0001's delete list. Undownloaded export files stay downloadable for up to 24 hours, which R4 doesn't justify, since they can be deleted. A failed number check keeps the previous summary, so a withdrawal's regeneration can fail silently.
- **Raw text in turn rows:** ADR 0001's delete list treats turn records as a separate store. They hold participant text and replies, yet the raw-text expiry rule names only the raw table.
- **Retention:** Q18 says retention is per collection, but no ADR describes it. Write-table history, including changed answers, has no end date. "Collection close" may never happen for a long-running survey.

**Change:** ADR 0001, "Withdrawal"; ADR 0004, "Withdrawal"; ADR 0005, "History" and "Executive summary". Add a retention table covering every store, with a maximum if a collection never closes.

**The other side:** purging quotes from chat history adds work, but deleting by source reference is straightforward. The summary effect from one withdrawal is small, though it should still be marked stale.

### 18. Redaction has weak gates
- **No gate on R3's test:** "test per language" has no consequence if a language fails. A failing language should keep its transcripts hidden, or require manual review.
- **Model replies:** a reply that repeats "Thanks, James" needs redacting too, but "after each AI answer" doesn't clearly include it.
- **Context:** per-message redaction loses context across turns, and mixed-language text isn't addressed.
- **Self-harm flag:** this may be the most sensitive field in the system, yet analysts see it under ordinary transcript permission, and ADR 0002's "can filter them out" may extend to it. It also isn't a "closed question", which is ADR 0004's rule for filter fields.

**Change:** ADR 0004, "Transcripts and redaction"; ADR 0003, "Refusals and safeguarding". Add a per-language pass/fail gate and redact both sides of the conversation. Keep the category out of filters, and restrict access to the turn.

**The other side:** clients with a safeguarding duty may need to see these flags fast. That's a reason for a named safeguarding role, not for wide access.

### 19. R9 has no go/no-go, though the design expects frequent flags
ADR 0003 says health surveys may trigger false positives often, so flagged health text stored for possible human review is a likely outcome. Approval is ASSUMED, and nothing says what happens if it's refused.

Azure prompt attack detection also has no stated data path or region, and if it runs as a separate resource it could add one.

**Change:** ADR 0002, "Models"; R9. Add approval to the region check in Q22, or have you accept the exposure explicitly and put it in the participant notice. Add the data path for prompt detection to ADR 0005's privacy table.

**The other side:** the storage stays in the geography, and Microsoft's terms may be acceptable to clients. That's a fine outcome, but it needs to be a recorded decision.

### 20. Export checks at the wrong time
The live control plane check isn't tied to a specific step. The file can sit for 24 hours after a request.

**Scenario:** an admin starts a row-level export, is removed, and fetches the SAS link later with a token still valid for up to 5 minutes. `GET .../exports/{id}` lists "Analyst; row-level admin", with no owner check, so a wrong implementation lets any admin take another admin's file.

**Change:** ADR 0004, "How it runs". Check again when issuing the SAS, bind each export to its requester, and audit each SAS issued. Confirm blob logging proves downloads, which assumptions.md lists as not checked.

**The other side:** allowing any admin to download a colleague's export may be useful, provided it's chosen and audited.

### 21. Client-created IDs are not scoped to a participant
ADR 0003 says "the same turn ID returns the existing turn". ADR 0001's unique key is collection plus client answer ID. If lookups aren't also scoped to the response, a known or reused ID returns someone else's turn or blocks their answer. Chat turn and stream reads have the same gap, as the guard list has no ownership check.

**Change:** ADR 0001, "Write table"; ADR 0003, "Who owns a turn"; ADR 0004, API guard. Scope uniqueness and reads to the response or user, and take that from the token.

**The other side:** random IDs make guessing impractical, so this is a low-probability fault. The fix is cheap.

## Low
- **Regional role copy (Q28 fallback):** if a push fails, a stale role has no time limit. Add a TTL and fail closed. Change in ADR 0002.
- **Control plane concentration:** one signing key can mint access to every region, so "stacks share no data" overstates isolation. Name where the key lives, and add this to risks.md. Better Auth key storage is unchecked.
- **Collection names in Sweden Central:** "HIV study, Nairobi" would be health context outside the region. R15 covers user details only. Use neutral names or IDs in the control plane, in ADR 0002.
- **WAF tuning order:** detection-mode tuning logs real answers, and scrubbing also hides what you need to tune. Enable scrubbing first, and tune on test data. Change in R8 and ADR 0002.
- **CSV escaping:** add leading tab and carriage return to the listed characters. Change in ADR 0004.
- **Backup location:** ADR 0002 says backups stay in the geography, but assumptions.md has no source for it. Geo-redundant backup settings need checking, particularly for South Africa, where the other region is restricted.

## What holds up
- Front Door static-only, with participant data decrypted only in the region.
- The fixed-tool chat design, with no write tools and plain-text output. A viewer's chat can't pull in transcripts.
- Aggregates-only summaries.
- Tokens in fragments and headers, and POST for filter values.
- The refusal to fail over outside the geography.
- Tombstones on withdrawal.
- The honest statements in R4, R5 and R16.

## Priority before design.md section 9
Fix 1 to 3 first, since they break the Q7 commitment. Then write the missing designs for 4 and 5. Findings 1, 2, 3 and 11 should also become test recipes in T4, which currently only says "tests that try to recover hidden values".

[Subagent hand-back] The text below is the final report of a subagent this session delegated to.
